White pawn is at c6.
Black pawn is at c7.
Wait, how can a white pawn be at c6 and a black pawn be at c7?
In chess, pawns move forward. White pawns move from rank 2 towards rank 8. Black pawns move from rank 7 towards rank 1.
If white pawn is at c6 and black pawn is at c7, they are face-to-face.
White pawn at c6: can move to c7 only if it's a capture. But c7 is occupied by a black pawn.
White pawn at c6 cannot move forward to c7.
Black pawn at c7: can move to c6 only if it's a capture. But c6 is occupied by a white pawn.
Black pawn at c7 cannot move forward to c6.
This is a blocked pawn structure on the c-file.

Queen at d8, target d6.
Yes, that's a straight line. `Qxd6`.

White pieces: c6 pawn. The pawn at c6 does not protect d6.

None protect d6.

`c8e7`
Target e7.
Queen at d8.
Yes, `Qxe7`.

No.
So `c8e7` is a blunder.

Pawn at a7. `Nxa7`.
Is the knight protected at a7?
No.
Is a7 defended?
Rook at a8. `Rxa7`.
So `c8a7` is a blunder.

Therefore, the only viable knight move that is not a blunder is `c8b6`.

`c1e3`, `c2c4`, `f2f3`, `g2g3`, `h2h3` etc.

Black could move the knight at e4.
If Black moves the knight from e4, the pressure on White's position decreases.
`c8b6` is a strong positional move.
